# South Africa

South Africa

Country in Southern Africa

This article is about the country. For the geographical area, see [Southern Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Africa "Southern Africa"). For other uses, see [South Africa (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa_(disambiguation) "South Africa (disambiguation)").

"Mzansi" redirects here. For other uses, see [Mzansi (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mzansi_(disambiguation) "Mzansi (disambiguation)").

For the republic between 1961 and 1984, see [Republic of South Africa (1961–1984)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_South_Africa_(1961%E2%80%931984) "Republic of South Africa (1961–1984)").

**South Africa**, officially the **Republic of South Africa** ( **RSA**), is the [southernmost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Africa "Southern Africa") country in [Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Africa "Africa").[\[d\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-19) The country consists of [nine provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_South_Africa "Provinces of South Africa"), and is bounded to the south by 2,798 kilometres (1,739 miles) of [coastline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coastline "Coastline") that stretches along the [South Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Ocean "Atlantic Ocean") and [Indian Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean "Indian Ocean");[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-20)[\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-21)[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-safacts-22) to the north by the neighbouring countries of [Namibia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namibia "Namibia"), [Botswana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botswana "Botswana"), and [Zimbabwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zimbabwe "Zimbabwe"); and to the east and northeast by [Mozambique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mozambique "Mozambique") and [Eswatini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eswatini "Eswatini"). South Africa also encloses [Lesotho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesotho "Lesotho").[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-23) Covering an area of 1,221,037 square kilometres (471,445 square miles), the country has [a population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_South_Africa "Demographics of South Africa") of over 63 million people, making it the [sixth-most populated country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_population "List of African countries by population") in Africa, and [24th-most](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population "List of countries and dependencies by population") in the world. [Pretoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pretoria "Pretoria") is the [administrative capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_city "Capital city"), while [Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Town "Cape Town"), as the seat of [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_South_Africa "Parliament of South Africa"), is the legislative capital, and [Bloemfontein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloemfontein "Bloemfontein") is regarded as the judicial capital.[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Marais_Twala_2020_pp._49%E2%80%9362-24) The largest and most populous city is [Johannesburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannesburg "Johannesburg"), followed by Cape Town and [Durban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durban "Durban").[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-25)

[Archaeological findings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cradle_of_Humankind "Cradle of Humankind") suggest that various [hominid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hominid "Hominid") species existed in South Africa about 2.5 million years ago, and [modern humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_humans "Modern humans") inhabited the region over 100,000 years ago.[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-26) The first known people were the indigenous [Khoisan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khoisan "Khoisan"), and [Bantu-speaking peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_peoples_of_South_Africa "Bantu peoples of South Africa"), who migrated in waves, from west and central Africa to the region 2,000 to 1,000 years ago.[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-gunnink1-27) In the north, the [Kingdom of Mapungubwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Mapungubwe "Kingdom of Mapungubwe") formed in the 13th century, and the [Venda Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venda_Kingdom "Venda Kingdom") in the 17th century. In 1652, the [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_colonial_empire "Dutch colonial empire") established the first European settlement at [Table Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Table_Bay "Table Bay"), [Dutch Cape Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Cape_Colony "Dutch Cape Colony"). Its [invasion in 1795](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_the_Cape_Colony "Invasion of the Cape Colony"), and the [Battle of Blaauwberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Blaauwberg "Battle of Blaauwberg") in 1806 led to British occupation. The [Mfecane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mfecane "Mfecane"), a period of significant upheaval, led to the formation of various African kingdoms, including the [Zulu Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulu_Kingdom "Zulu Kingdom"). The region was further colonised, and the [Mineral Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mineral_Revolution "Mineral Revolution") saw a shift towards industrialisation and urbanisation. Following the [Second Boer War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Boer_War "Second Boer War"), the [Union of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_South_Africa "Union of South Africa") was [created in 1910](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa_Act_1909 "South Africa Act 1909") after the amalgamation of the [Cape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Colony "Cape Colony"), [Natal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_Natal "Colony of Natal"), [Transvaal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transvaal_Colony "Transvaal Colony"), and [Orange River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_River_Colony "Orange River Colony") colonies, becoming a [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic "Republic") after the [1961 referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_South_African_republic_referendum "1960 South African republic referendum"). The multi-racial [Cape Qualified Franchise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Qualified_Franchise "Cape Qualified Franchise") in the Cape was gradually eroded, and the vast majority of [Black South Africans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_South_Africans "Black South Africans") were not [enfranchised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suffrage "Suffrage") until 1994.[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-gunnink1-27)

The [National Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Party_(South_Africa) "National Party (South Africa)") imposed [apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid "Apartheid") in 1948, institutionalising previous [racial segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation "Racial segregation"). After a [struggle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_resistance_to_apartheid "Internal resistance to apartheid") by the [African National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_National_Congress "African National Congress") and other [anti-apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Apartheid_Movement "Anti-Apartheid Movement") activists, both inside and outside the country, the repeal of discriminatory laws began in the mid-1980s. Universal elections [took place in 1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_South_African_general_election "1994 South African general election"), following which all racial groups have held political representation in the country's [liberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy "Liberal democracy"), which comprises a [parliamentary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_republic "Parliamentary republic") and nine provinces. Recognised as a [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power "Middle power") in international affairs, South Africa maintains significant [regional influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_power "Regional power") and is a member of [BRICS+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BRICS+ "BRICS+"), the [African Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Union "African Union") (hosting the seat of the [Pan-African Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-African_Parliament "Pan-African Parliament")), [SADC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_African_Development_Community "Southern African Development Community"), [SACU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_African_Customs_Union "Southern African Customs Union"), the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations "Commonwealth of Nations"), and the [G20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G20 "G20").[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-28)[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Lynch2010-29)

A [developing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developing_country "Developing country") and [newly industrialised country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newly_industrialised_country "Newly industrialised country"), it has the [largest economy in Africa by nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_GDP_(nominal) "List of African countries by GDP (nominal)"),[\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-30)[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Waugh2000-31) is tied with [Ethiopia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopia "Ethiopia") for the most [UNESCO World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Africa "List of World Heritage Sites in Africa") in [Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Africa "Africa"),[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-:10-32) and is a [biodiversity hotspot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_hotspot "Biodiversity hotspot") with unique [biomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomes "Biomes"), plant, and animal life. South Africa encompasses [a variety of cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_South_Africa "Culture of South Africa"), [languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_South_Africa "Languages of South Africa"), and [religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_South_Africa "Religion in South Africa"), and has been called the " [rainbow nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainbow_nation "Rainbow nation")", especially in the wake of apartheid, to describe its diversity.[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-33) Since the end of apartheid, government accountability and [quality of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quality_of_life "Quality of life") have substantially improved for non-white citizens.[\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-34) However, [crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_in_South_Africa "Crime in South Africa"), violence, [poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_South_Africa "Poverty in South Africa"), and [inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inequality_in_post-apartheid_South_Africa "Inequality in post-apartheid South Africa") remain widespread, with about 32% of the population unemployed as of 2024[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=South_Africa&action=edit),[\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-35)[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-36) while some 56% lived below the [poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty "Poverty") line in 2014.[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-37)[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-worldbank-poverty-38) Having the highest [Gini coefficient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gini_coefficient "Gini coefficient") of 0.67, South Africa is considered one of the most economically unequal countries in the world.[\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-39)[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-40)

## Etymology

See also: [List of official names of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_official_names_of_South_Africa "List of official names of South Africa")

The name "South Africa" is derived from the country's geographic location at the southern tip of Africa. Upon formation, the country was named the [Union of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_South_Africa "Union of South Africa") in English and _Unie van Zuid-Afrika_ in [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language "Dutch language"), reflecting its origin from the unification of four British colonies. Since 1961, the long formal name in English has been the "Republic of South Africa" and _Republiek van Suid-Afrika_ in [Afrikaans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrikaans "Afrikaans"). The country has an official name in [12 official languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_South_Africa "Languages of South Africa").[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-41)[\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-42)

**Mzansi**, derived from the [Xhosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xhosa_language "Xhosa language") noun _uMzantsi_ meaning "south", is a [colloquial name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colloquialism "Colloquialism") for South Africa,[\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-43)[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-44) while some [Pan-Africanist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Africanism "Pan-Africanism") political parties prefer the term " [Azania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azania "Azania")", which is a word originating from Greek, and not an African language.[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Azania-45)

## History

Main article: [History of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_South_Africa "History of South Africa")

### Prehistory

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/Maropeng_visitor_centre%2C_Cradle_of_Humankind%2C_South_Africa.jpg/500px-Maropeng_visitor_centre%2C_Cradle_of_Humankind%2C_South_Africa.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Maropeng_visitor_centre,_Cradle_of_Humankind,_South_Africa.jpg) Front of Maropeng at the [Cradle of Humankind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cradle_of_Humankind "Cradle of Humankind")

South Africa contains some of the oldest archaeological and [human-fossil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_human_evolution_fossils "List of human evolution fossils") sites in the world.[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-46)[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-47)[\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-48) Archaeologists have recovered extensive fossil remains from a series of caves in [Gauteng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauteng "Gauteng") known as the [Cradle of Humankind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cradle_of_Humankind "Cradle of Humankind"), which has been declared a [UNESCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO "UNESCO") [World Heritage Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Site "World Heritage Site"). The sites include [Sterkfontein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sterkfontein "Sterkfontein"), one of the richest sites for [hominin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hominin "Hominin") fossils in the world, as well as [Swartkrans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swartkrans "Swartkrans"), [Gondolin Cave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gondolin_Cave "Gondolin Cave"), [Kromdraai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kromdraai_fossil_site "Kromdraai fossil site"), [Cooper's Cave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooper's_Cave "Cooper's Cave") and [Malapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malapa_Fossil_Site,_Cradle_of_Humankind "Malapa Fossil Site, Cradle of Humankind"). The first hominin fossil discovered in Africa, the [Taung Child](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taung_Child "Taung Child"), was found near [Taung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taung "Taung") in 1924. Other hominin remains have come from the sites of [Makapansgat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makapansgat "Makapansgat") in [Limpopo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limpopo "Limpopo"); [Cornelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornelia,_Free_State "Cornelia, Free State") and [Florisbad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florisbad "Florisbad") in the [Free State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_State_(province) "Free State (province)"); [Border Cave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Cave "Border Cave") in [KwaZulu-Natal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KwaZulu-Natal "KwaZulu-Natal"); [Klasies River Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klasies_River_Caves "Klasies River Caves") in the [Eastern Cape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Cape "Eastern Cape"); and [Pinnacle Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinnacle_Point "Pinnacle Point"), [Elandsfontein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saldanha_man "Saldanha man") and Die Kelders Cave in the [Western Cape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Cape "Western Cape").[\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-49)

These finds suggest that various hominid species existed in South Africa from about three million years ago, starting with _[Australopithecus africanus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australopithecus_africanus "Australopithecus africanus"),_[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-50) followed by _[Australopithecus sediba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australopithecus_sediba "Australopithecus sediba")_, _[Homo ergaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_ergaster "Homo ergaster")_, _[Homo erectus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_erectus "Homo erectus")_, _[Homo rhodesiensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_rhodesiensis "Homo rhodesiensis")_, _[Homo helmei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_helmei "Homo helmei")_, _[Homo naledi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_naledi "Homo naledi")_ and modern [humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human "Human") ( _Homo sapiens_). Modern humans have inhabited Southern Africa for at least 170,000 years. Various researchers have located [pebble tools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oldowan "Oldowan") within the [Vaal River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaal_River "Vaal River") valley.[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Langer-51)[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-52)

### Pre-colonial period

Main article: [Bantu expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_expansion "Bantu expansion")

The indigenous [San](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_people "San people") and [Khoikhoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khoikhoi "Khoikhoi") peoples of Southern Africa were predominantly [hunter-gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherers "Hunter-gatherers") and, in the case of the Khoikhoi, also practised [pastoralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastoralism "Pastoralism").[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-53) [Khoisan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khoisan "Khoisan") peoples may be the descendants of an early dispersal of [anatomically modern humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatomically_modern_humans "Anatomically modern humans") to Southern Africa before 150,000years ago.[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-54) They were largely displaced or absorbed by the [Bantu expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_expansion "Bantu expansion") which took place between 1,500 and 2,000years ago.[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-55)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/MapungubweHill.jpg/500px-MapungubweHill.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:MapungubweHill.jpg) [Mapungubwe Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mapungubwe_Hill "Mapungubwe Hill"), the site of the former capital of the [Kingdom of Mapungubwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Mapungubwe "Kingdom of Mapungubwe")

Bantu [settlers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Settlers "Settlers") expanded from West Africa from approximately 3,000 BCE.[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-56) Settlements of [Bantu-speaking peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_peoples_of_South_Africa "Bantu peoples of South Africa"), who were iron-using agriculturists and [herdsmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herder "Herder"), were present south of the [Limpopo River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limpopo_River "Limpopo River") (now the northern border with [Botswana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botswana "Botswana") and [Zimbabwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zimbabwe "Zimbabwe")) by the 4th or 5th century. The earliest [ironworks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ironworks "Ironworks") in modern-day KwaZulu-Natal are believed to date from around 1050.[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-57) The southernmost group was the [Xhosa people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xhosa_people "Xhosa people"), whose language incorporates certain linguistic traits from the Khoisan languages, and who eventually reached the [Great Fish River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Fish_River "Great Fish River") in the present-day Eastern Cape. As they migrated, these larger [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age "Iron Age") populations displaced or assimilated earlier peoples. In [Mpumalanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mpumalanga "Mpumalanga"), several stone circles have been found along with a stone arrangement that has been named [Adam's Calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blaauboschkraal_stone_ruins "Blaauboschkraal stone ruins"), and the ruins are thought to be created by the [Bakone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bokoni "Bokoni"), a [Northern Sotho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Sotho_language "Northern Sotho language") group.[\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-58)[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-59)

Around 1220, in the [Limpopo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limpopo_River "Limpopo River")- [Shashe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shashe_River "Shashe River") Basin, the elite of [K2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bambandyanalo "Bambandyanalo") moved to settle the flat-topped summit of Mapungubwe Hill, with the population settling below. [Rainmaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainmaking_(ritual) "Rainmaking (ritual)") was crucial to the development of [sacral kingship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacral_kingship "Sacral kingship"), and several rainmaking sites have been found nearby including [Ratho Kroonkop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ratho_Kroonkop "Ratho Kroonkop"). By 1250, the capital of the [Mapungubwe Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mapungubwe_Kingdom "Mapungubwe Kingdom") had a population of 5,000 and the state covered 30,000km2 (11,500 square miles), growing wealthy through the [Indian Ocean trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean_trade "Indian Ocean trade"). The events around Mapungubwe's collapse circa 1300 are unknown; however, trade routes shifted north from the Limpopo to the [Zambezi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zambezi "Zambezi"), precipitating the rise of [Great Zimbabwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Zimbabwe "Great Zimbabwe"). The hill was abandoned and Mapungubwe's population scattered.[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-60)

In the [Soutpansberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soutpansberg "Soutpansberg"), interactions between early [Shona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shona_people "Shona people") inhabitants (possible from Mapungubwe), late-comer Shona and [Sotho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sotho_people "Sotho people")-speakers between the 15th and 17th centuries culminated in the formation of the [Venda language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venda_language "Venda language") and identity.[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-:13-61) In the late-17th century, a splinter group from the dynasty of the [Rozvi Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rozvi_Empire "Rozvi Empire") migrated south; they were the Singo. The Singo settled at [Dzata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzata "Dzata"), which became the capital of the [Venda Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venda_Kingdom "Venda Kingdom"), and came to subdue all of the Soutpansberg. In the late-18th century, following trade routes moving south, the state collapsed. The most powerful dynasties that remained were the Ramabulana Singo in the western Soutpansberg, and the Tshivhase Singo and Mphaphuli Singo in the eastern Soutpansberg.[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-:11-62)

### Portuguese exploration

See also: [Portuguese discoveries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_discoveries "Portuguese discoveries")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/F._Benda-The_planting_of_cross_by_Bartholomew_Dias_in_1488-0681_%28cropped%29.jpg/500px-F._Benda-The_planting_of_cross_by_Bartholomew_Dias_in_1488-0681_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F._Benda-The_planting_of_cross_by_Bartholomew_Dias_in_1488-0681_(cropped).jpg) Portuguese explorer [Bartolomeu Dias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bartolomeu_Dias "Bartolomeu Dias") planting the cross at [Cape Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Point "Cape Point") after being the first to successfully round the [Cape of Good Hope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_of_Good_Hope "Cape of Good Hope")

In 1487, the Portuguese explorer [Bartolomeu Dias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bartolomeu_Dias "Bartolomeu Dias") led the first European voyage to land in southern Africa.[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-domville-25-63) On 4 December, he landed at Walfisch Bay (now known as [Walvis Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walvis_Bay "Walvis Bay") in present-day Namibia). This was south of the furthest point reached in 1485 by his predecessor, the Portuguese navigator [Diogo Cão](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diogo_C%C3%A3o "Diogo Cão"), being [Cape Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Cross "Cape Cross"), north of the bay. Dias continued down the western coast of southern Africa. After 8 January 1488, prevented by storms from proceeding along the coast, he sailed out of sight of land and passed the southernmost point of Africa without seeing it. He reached as far up the eastern coast of Africa as, what he called, _Rio do Infante_, probably the present-day [Groot River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groot_River_(Eastern_Cape) "Groot River (Eastern Cape)"), in May 1488. On his return, he saw the cape, which he named _Cabo das Tormentas_ ('Cape of Storms'). King [John II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_II_of_Portugal "John II of Portugal") renamed the point _Cabo da Boa Esperança_, or [Cape of Good Hope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_of_Good_Hope "Cape of Good Hope"), as it led to the riches of the [East Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Indies "East Indies").[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-64) Dias' feat of navigation was immortalised in [Luís de Camões](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lu%C3%ADs_de_Cam%C3%B5es "Luís de Camões")' 1572 epic poem, _[Os Lusíadas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Os_Lus%C3%ADadas "Os Lusíadas")_.[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-65)

In 1497 [Vasco Da Gama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasco_Da_Gama "Vasco Da Gama") set sail from Lisbon and became the first European to reach India in 1498 via the Cape of Good Hope, opening up oceanic trade between Europe and Asia. He bypassed the coast of present-day South Africa and made landfall in present-day Mozambique.[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-66)

### Dutch and British colonisation of the Cape

Main articles: [Dutch Cape Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Cape_Colony "Dutch Cape Colony"), [Slavery in South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_South_Africa "Slavery in South Africa"), and [Cape Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Colony "Cape Colony")

The Dutch first made contact with the coast of Southern Africa in 1595. With Portuguese maritime power declining in the early 17th century, English and Dutch merchants competed to dislodge Portugal's lucrative monopoly on the [spice trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spice_trade "Spice trade").[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Pakeman-67) British [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company "East India Company") representatives sporadically called at the Cape in search of provisions from as early as 1601 but later came to favour [Ascension Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ascension_Island "Ascension Island") and [Saint Helena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Helena "Saint Helena") as ports of refuge.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Kaap-68) Dutch interest was aroused after 1647, when two employees of the [Dutch East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company "Dutch East India Company") were shipwrecked at the Cape for several months, and managed to survive by obtaining fresh water and meat from the indigenous peoples,[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Kaap-68) and sowing crops in the fertile soil.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-zastudy-69) Upon their return to Holland, they reported favourably on the Cape's potential as a "warehouse and garden" for provisions to stock passing ships for long voyages.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Kaap-68)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Charles_Bell_-_Jan_van_Riebeeck_se_aankoms_aan_die_Kaap.jpg/500px-Charles_Bell_-_Jan_van_Riebeeck_se_aankoms_aan_die_Kaap.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Charles_Bell_-_Jan_van_Riebeeck_se_aankoms_aan_die_Kaap.jpg) [Charles Davidson Bell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Davidson_Bell "Charles Davidson Bell")'s 19th-century painting of [Jan van Riebeeck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_van_Riebeeck "Jan van Riebeeck"), who founded the first European settlement in South Africa, arriving in [Table Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Table_Bay "Table Bay") in 1652

In 1652, a century and a half after the discovery of the [Cape Route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Route "Cape Route"), [Jan van Riebeeck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_van_Riebeeck "Jan van Riebeeck") established a _[victualling](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/victual#English "wikt:victual")_ station at the Cape of Good Hope, at what would become [Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Town "Cape Town"), on behalf of the Dutch East India Company.[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-70)[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Hunt1-71) In time, the Cape became home to a large population of [Free Burghers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Burghers_in_the_Dutch_Cape_Colony "Free Burghers in the Dutch Cape Colony").[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Hunt1-71) Dutch traders also brought thousands of [enslaved people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery "Slavery") to the fledgling colony from present-day Indonesia, Madagascar, and eastern Africa.[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Worden-72) Some of the earliest mixed race communities in the country were formed between Free Burghers, enslaved people, and indigenous peoples.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-zimstudy-73) This led to the development of a new ethnic group, the [Cape Coloureds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Coloureds "Cape Coloureds"), most of whom adopted the Dutch language and Christian faith.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-zimstudy-73) Conflicts over resources between the indigenous Khoisan people and Dutch settlers began in the 17th century and continued for centuries.[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-74)

Dutch colonists' eastward expansion caused [wars with the southwesterly migrating Xhosa nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xhosa_Wars "Xhosa Wars"), as both sides competed for the pastureland near the Great Fish River, which the colonists desired for grazing cattle.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Stapleton2-75) Free Burghers who became independent farmers on the frontier were known as _[Boers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boers "Boers")_, with some (the _[trekboers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trekboers "Trekboers")_) adopting semi-nomadic lifestyles.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Stapleton2-75) The Boers formed loose [militias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Militia "Militia"), which they termed _commandos_, and forged alliances with Khoisan peoples to repel Xhosa raids.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Stapleton2-75) Both sides launched bloody but inconclusive offensives, and sporadic violence, often accompanied by livestock theft, remained common for several decades.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Stapleton2-75)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Charles_Bell_-_Zoeloe-aanval_op_%27n_Boerelaer_-_1838.jpg/500px-Charles_Bell_-_Zoeloe-aanval_op_%27n_Boerelaer_-_1838.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Charles_Bell_-_Zoeloe-aanval_op_'n_Boerelaer_-_1838.jpg) Depiction of a [Zulu attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weenen_massacre "Weenen massacre") on a Boer camp in February 1838

Great Britain occupied Cape Town between 1795 and 1803 to prevent it from falling under the control of the [French First Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_First_Republic "French First Republic"), which had invaded the [Low Countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_Countries "Low Countries").[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Stapleton2-75) After briefly returning to Dutch rule under the [Batavian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batavian_Republic "Batavian Republic") in 1803, the Cape was occupied again by the British in 1806.[\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Keegan-76) Following the end of the [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars "Napoleonic Wars"), it was formally ceded to Great Britain and became an integral part of the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire "British Empire").[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Lloyd1-77) British emigration to South Africa began around 1818, culminating in the arrival of the [1820 Settlers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1820_Settlers "1820 Settlers"),[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Lloyd1-77) the purpose of which was primarily to increase the size of the European workforce and bolster frontier regions against Xhosa incursions.[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Lloyd1-77)

### Mfecane and colonial expansion

Main articles: [Cape Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Colony "Cape Colony"), [Mfecane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mfecane "Mfecane"), [Boer Republics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boer_Republics "Boer Republics"), and [Great Trek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Trek "Great Trek")

In the early 1800s, the [Mfecane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mfecane "Mfecane") (lit.'crushing') saw a heightened period of conflict, migration, and state formation among native groups, caused by the complex interplay of international trade, environmental instability, and European colonisation.[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-78) Chiefdoms grew wealthier and competed over trade routes and grazing land, leading to the formation of the [Ndwandwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ndwandwe "Ndwandwe") and [Mthethwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mthethwa_Kingdom "Mthethwa Kingdom") Paramountcies in the east of the country.[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-79) Ndwandwe defeated Mthethwa, which in turn split into several groups, one of which was led by [Shaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaka "Shaka") of the [amaZulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AmaZulu "AmaZulu").[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-T5-80) The 1810s saw the fourth and fifth [Xhosa Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xhosa_Wars "Xhosa Wars") as British colonisation expanded.[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-81) The Ndwandwe Paramountcy splintered amid costly raids, and Shaka's [Zulu Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulu_Kingdom "Zulu Kingdom") rose to fill the power vacuum.[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-T5-80) The [Gaza Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_Empire "Gaza Empire") concurrently formed, and while the Zulu managed to totally defeat the Ndwandwe, they were repelled by Gaza.[\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-82)[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-83)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Cape_Colony_map_1876_-_Eve_of_Confederation_Wars.jpg/500px-Cape_Colony_map_1876_-_Eve_of_Confederation_Wars.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cape_Colony_map_1876_-_Eve_of_Confederation_Wars.jpg) 1876 map of South Africa

During the early 19th century, many Dutch settlers departed from the [Cape Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Colony "Cape Colony"), where they had been subjected to British control, in a series of migrant groups who came to be known as _[Voortrekkers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voortrekkers "Voortrekkers")_ (lit.'pathfinders' or 'pioneers'). They migrated to the future [Natal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natal_(province) "Natal (province)"), Free State, and [Transvaal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transvaal_(province) "Transvaal (province)") regions, and following their victory over the Zulu Kingdom at the [Battle of Blood River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Blood_River "Battle of Blood River") on 16 December 1838, founded the [Boer republics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boer_republics "Boer republics"): the [Natalia Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natalia_Republic "Natalia Republic"), the [South African Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_Republic "South African Republic") and the [Orange Free State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Free_State "Orange Free State").[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-84) In the interior, the Cape Colony expanded at the expense of the [Batswana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tswana_people "Tswana people") and [Griqua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Griqua_people "Griqua people"), and Boer expansion destabilised the middle Orange River region.[\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-85) The [Matabele kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matabele_kingdom "Matabele kingdom") came to dominate the eastern interior, and raided the [Venda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venda_people "Venda people") kingdom.[\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-86)

The discovery of diamonds in 1867 and gold in 1884 in the interior initiated the [Mineral Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mineral_Revolution "Mineral Revolution"), which increased economic growth and immigration and intensified British subjugation of the indigenous peoples. The struggle to control these important economic resources shaped relations between Europeans and the indigenous population, as well as between the Boers and the British.[\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-87)

On 16 May 1876, President [Thomas François Burgers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Fran%C3%A7ois_Burgers "Thomas François Burgers") of the South African Republic declared war against the [Pedi people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedi_people "Pedi people"). [King Sekhukhune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sekhukhune "Sekhukhune") defeated the army on 1 August 1876, with another attack by the Lydenburg Volunteer Corps being similarly repulsed. On 16 February 1877, the two parties signed a peace treaty at [Botshabelo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botshabelo,_Mpumalanga "Botshabelo, Mpumalanga").[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-88) The Boers' inability to subdue the Pedi led to the departure of Burgers in favour of [Paul Kruger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Kruger "Paul Kruger") and the British annexation of the South African Republic. In 1878 and 1879, three British attacks were successfully repelled until [Garnet Wolseley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garnet_Wolseley,_1st_Viscount_Wolseley "Garnet Wolseley, 1st Viscount Wolseley") defeated Sekhukhune in November 1879 with an army of 2,000 British soldiers, Boers and 10,000 Swazis.

The [Anglo-Zulu War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Zulu_War "Anglo-Zulu War") was fought in 1879 between the British and the [Zulu Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulu_Kingdom "Zulu Kingdom"). Following [Lord Carnarvon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Herbert,_4th_Earl_of_Carnarvon "Henry Herbert, 4th Earl of Carnarvon")'s successful introduction of [federation in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Confederation "Canadian Confederation"), it was thought that similar political effort, coupled with military campaigns, might succeed with the African kingdoms, tribal areas and Boer republics in South Africa. In 1874, [Henry Bartle Frere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Bartle_Frere "Henry Bartle Frere") was sent to South Africa as the British [High Commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Commissioner_for_Southern_Africa "High Commissioner for Southern Africa") to bring such plans into being. Among the obstacles were the presence of the independent states of the Boers, and the Zulu army. The Zulu nation defeated the British at the [Battle of Isandlwana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Isandlwana "Battle of Isandlwana") but ultimately lost the war, resulting in the termination of its independence.[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-89)

### Boer Wars and independence

Main articles: [Boer Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boer_Wars "Boer Wars") and [Union of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_South_Africa "Union of South Africa")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Battle_of_Majuba_Hill.jpg/500px-Battle_of_Majuba_Hill.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Battle_of_Majuba_Hill.jpg) The [Battle of Majuba Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Majuba_Hill "Battle of Majuba Hill") was the last decisive battle during the [First Boer War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Boer_War "First Boer War").

The Boer republics successfully resisted British encroachments during the [First Boer War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Boer_War "First Boer War") (1880–1881) using [guerrilla warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guerrilla_warfare "Guerrilla warfare") tactics, which were well-suited to local conditions. The British returned with greater numbers, more experience, and new strategy in the [Second Boer War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Boer_War "Second Boer War") (1899–1902) and, although suffering heavy casualties due to Boer [attrition warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attrition_warfare "Attrition warfare"), were ultimately successful due in part to [scorched earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scorched_earth "Scorched earth") tactics and [concentration camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Boer_War_concentration_camps "Second Boer War concentration camps"), in which 27,000 Boer civilians died due to a combination of disease and neglect.[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-90) South Africa's urban population grew rapidly from the end of the 19th century onward. After the devastation of the wars, Boer farmers fled into [Transvaal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transvaal_Colony "Transvaal Colony") and Orange Free State cities, many of whom would come to constitute an urban class of " [poor whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poor_whites "Poor whites")".[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Ogura_1996-91)

Anti-British policies among white South Africans focused on independence. During the Dutch and British colonial years, [racial segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation "Racial segregation") was mostly informal, though some legislation was enacted to control the settlement and movement of indigenous people, including the [Native Location Act of 1879](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Location_Act_of_1879 "Native Location Act of 1879") and the system of [pass laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pass_laws "Pass laws").[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-92)[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-93)[\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-94)[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-95)[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-96)

Eight years after the end of the Second Boer War, and after four years of negotiation, the [South Africa Act 1909](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa_Act_1909 "South Africa Act 1909") created the [Union of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_South_Africa "Union of South Africa") on 31 May 1910, granting the country nominal independence. The union was a [dominion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion "Dominion") that included the former territories of the Cape, Transvaal and Natal colonies, as well as the Orange Free State republic.[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-97) The [Natives Land Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natives_Land_Act,_1913 "Natives Land Act, 1913") of 1913, which was passed by the British ruling the Parliament of the newly formed Union of South Africa, severely restricted the ownership of land by [black South Africans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_South_Africans "Black South Africans"), who at that stage controlled only 7% of the country. The amount of land reserved for indigenous peoples was later marginally increased.[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-98)

In 1931, the union became fully sovereign from the United Kingdom with the passage of the [Statute of Westminster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Westminster_1931 "Statute of Westminster 1931"), which abolished the last powers of the [Parliament of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_the_United_Kingdom "Parliament of the United Kingdom") to legislate in the country. In 1934, the [South African Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_Party "South African Party") and [National Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Party_(South_Africa) "National Party (South Africa)") merged to form the [United Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Party_(South_Africa) "United Party (South Africa)"), seeking reconciliation between [Afrikaners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrikaner "Afrikaner") and English-speaking whites. In 1939, the party split over the entry of the Union into [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II "World War II") as an ally of the United Kingdom, a move which National Party followers opposed.[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-99)

### Apartheid era

Main articles: [Apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid "Apartheid"), [History of South Africa (1948–1994)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_South_Africa_(1948%E2%80%931994) "History of South Africa (1948–1994)"), and [Republic of South Africa (1961–1984)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_South_Africa_(1961%E2%80%931984) "Republic of South Africa (1961–1984)")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/DurbanSign1989.jpg/500px-DurbanSign1989.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:DurbanSign1989.jpg) A trilingual apartheid-era beach sign ( [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language "English language"), [Afrikaans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrikaans "Afrikaans"), [isiZulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulu_language "Zulu language")) stating that the bathing area was reserved for the "white race group", Durban, 1989

In 1948, the National Party was elected to power. It strengthened the racial segregation begun under Dutch and British colonial rule. Taking Canada's [Indian Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Act "Indian Act") as a framework,[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-100) the [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism "Nationalism") government classified all peoples into three races (being "Whites", "Blacks", and "Indians and Coloured people") and developed rights and limitations for each. The white-led [minoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minoritarian "Minoritarian") government[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-101) instituted a policy of legally institutionalised segregation which came to be known as _[apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid "Apartheid")_. While whites enjoyed a higher [standard of living](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_of_living "Standard of living") comparable to [First World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World "First World") Western nations, the black majority remained disadvantaged by almost every standard, including income, education, housing, and life expectancy.[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-102) The [Freedom Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Charter "Freedom Charter"), adopted in 1955 by the [Congress Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_Alliance "Congress Alliance"), which was multi-racial in makeup, demanded a non-racial society and an end to discrimination.

On 31 May 1961, the country became a republic following [a referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_South_African_republic_referendum "1960 South African republic referendum") (only open to white voters) which narrowly passed;[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-103) the British-dominated Natal province largely voted against the proposal. [Elizabeth II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_II "Elizabeth II") lost the title [Queen of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_South_Africa "Monarchy of South Africa"), and the last [Governor-General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_South_Africa "Governor-General of South Africa"), [Charles Robberts Swart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._R._Swart "C. R. Swart"), became [state president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_President_of_South_Africa "State President of South Africa"). As a concession to the [Westminster system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_system "Westminster system"), the appointment of the president remained by parliament, and the position was virtually powerless until [P. W. Botha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P._W._Botha "P. W. Botha")'s [Constitution Act of 1983](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_Constitution_of_1983 "South African Constitution of 1983"), which eliminated the office of [prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_South_Africa "Prime Minister of South Africa") and instated a unique "strong presidency" [responsible to parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system "Parliamentary system"). Pressured by other [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations "Commonwealth of Nations") countries, South Africa withdrew from the organisation in 1961.

Despite [opposition to apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_resistance_to_apartheid "Internal resistance to apartheid") both within and outside the country from all racial backgrounds, the government legislated for a continuation of apartheid. The security forces cracked down on internal dissent, and violence became widespread, with anti-apartheid organisations such as the [African National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_National_Congress "African National Congress") (ANC), the [Azanian People's Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azanian_People's_Organisation "Azanian People's Organisation"), and the [Pan-Africanist Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan_Africanist_Congress_of_Azania "Pan Africanist Congress of Azania") carrying out guerrilla warfare, urban sabotage and, according to [Oxford Academic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford_Academic "Oxford Academic"), [acts of terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism "Terrorism").[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Switzer-104)[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-105)[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-106) The three rival resistance movements also engaged in occasional inter-factional clashes as they jockeyed for domestic influence.[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Mitchell-107) Apartheid became increasingly controversial, and several countries began to boycott business with the South African government because of its racial policies. The boycotts and restrictions were later extended to international sanctions and the [divestment of holdings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disinvestment_from_South_Africa "Disinvestment from South Africa") by foreign investors.[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Bridgland-108)[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Landgren-109)

### Post-apartheid

Further information: [History of South Africa (1994–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_South_Africa_(1994%E2%80%93present) "History of South Africa (1994–present)")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Frederik_de_Klerk_with_Nelson_Mandela_-_World_Economic_Forum_Annual_Meeting_Davos_1992.jpg/500px-Frederik_de_Klerk_with_Nelson_Mandela_-_World_Economic_Forum_Annual_Meeting_Davos_1992.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Frederik_de_Klerk_with_Nelson_Mandela_-_World_Economic_Forum_Annual_Meeting_Davos_1992.jpg) [F.W. de Klerk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F._W._de_Klerk "F. W. de Klerk") and [Nelson Mandela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Mandela "Nelson Mandela") shake hands in January 1992.

The [Mahlabatini Declaration of Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahlabatini_Declaration_of_Faith "Mahlabatini Declaration of Faith"), signed by [Mangosuthu Buthelezi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangosuthu_Buthelezi "Mangosuthu Buthelezi") and [Harry Schwarz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Schwarz "Harry Schwarz") in 1974, enshrined the principles of [peaceful transition of power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peaceful_transition_of_power "Peaceful transition of power") and equality for all, the first of such agreements by black and white political leaders in South Africa.[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-110) Ultimately, [F.W. de Klerk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F._W._de_Klerk "F. W. de Klerk") opened bilateral discussions with [Nelson Mandela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Mandela "Nelson Mandela") in 1993 for a transition of policies and government.

In 1990, the National Party government took the first step towards dismantling apartheid when it lifted the ban on the ANC and other political organisations. It released Nelson Mandela from prison after 27 years of serving a sentence for sabotage and treason. A [negotiation process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negotiations_to_end_apartheid_in_South_Africa "Negotiations to end apartheid in South Africa") followed. With approval from the white electorate in a [1992 referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_South_African_apartheid_referendum "1992 South African apartheid referendum"), the government continued negotiations to end apartheid. South Africa held its first universal [elections in 1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_South_African_general_election "1994 South African general election"), which the ANC won by an overwhelming majority. The country rejoined the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations "Commonwealth of Nations") and became a member of the [Southern African Development Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_African_Development_Community "Southern African Development Community").[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-111)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/Watching_South_Africa_%26_France_match_at_World_Cup_2010-06-22_in_Soweto_13.jpg/500px-Watching_South_Africa_%26_France_match_at_World_Cup_2010-06-22_in_Soweto_13.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Watching_South_Africa_&_France_match_at_World_Cup_2010-06-22_in_Soweto_13.jpg) Supporters watching the [2010 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_FIFA_World_Cup "2010 FIFA World Cup") with [vuvuzelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vuvuzela "Vuvuzela") in the [township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Township_(South_Africa) "Township (South Africa)") of [Soweto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soweto "Soweto"), a [suburb of Johannesburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburbs_of_Johannesburg "Suburbs of Johannesburg")

The post-apartheid period, whilst plagued by high unemployment and increasing income inequality,[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-112)[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-113)[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-sach3-114) has been characterised by relative political stability. The country has, however, seen periodic outbreaks of violence, most notably the [2008 xenophobic riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_2008_South_Africa_riots "May 2008 South Africa riots")—during which over 60 people were killed,[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Broke-115) and an estimated 100,000 people were driven from their homes[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-116)—and the [Marikana massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marikana_massacre "Marikana massacre"), which constituted the most lethal use of force by South African security forces against civilians since the Soweto uprising.[\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-WashPo-117) Widespread [political corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_South_Africa "Corruption in South Africa") and [state capture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_capture "State capture") took place under the [presidency of Jacob Zuma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Jacob_Zuma "Presidency of Jacob Zuma"),[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-118)[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-119)[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-120)[\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-121)[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-PARI-Zondo-2022-122) whose brief imprisonment for [contempt of court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contempt_of_court "Contempt of court") during the course of his [trial for corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_Zuma_corruption_charges "Jacob Zuma corruption charges") in 2021 led to [widespread unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_South_African_unrest "2021 South African unrest") which left 354 people dead.[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-123)

The [electoral dominance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominant_party_system "Dominant party system") of the ANC has gradually declined since the early 2000s, with the party failing to secure a parliamentary majority for the first time in the [2024 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_South_African_general_election "2024 South African general election").[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-124) President [Cyril Ramaphosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyril_Ramaphosa "Cyril Ramaphosa") subsequently formed the [Government of National Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_cabinet_of_Cyril_Ramaphosa "Third cabinet of Cyril Ramaphosa") with, amongst others, the [Democratic Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Alliance_(South_Africa) "Democratic Alliance (South Africa)").[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-125)

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_South_Africa "Geography of South Africa")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/South_Africa_sat.jpg/500px-South_Africa_sat.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:South_Africa_sat.jpg) [Satellite image](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_image "Satellite image") of South Africa

South Africa is in [southern Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Africa "Southern Africa"), with a coastline that stretches more than 2,500km (1,553mi) and along two oceans—the [South Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Atlantic "South Atlantic") and the [Indian Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean "Indian Ocean"). At 1,219,912km2 (471,011sqmi) in area,[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-126) South Africa is the 24th-largest country in the world.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-127) Excluding the [Prince Edward Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Edward_Islands "Prince Edward Islands"), the country lies between latitudes [22°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/22nd_parallel_south "22nd parallel south") and [35°S](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/35th_parallel_south "35th parallel south"), and longitudes [16°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_meridian_east "16th meridian east") and [33°E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/33rd_meridian_east "33rd meridian east"). The interior of South Africa consists of a large, and in most places almost flat, [plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plateau "Plateau") with an altitude of between 1,000m (3,300ft) and 2,100m (6,900ft). It is highest in the east and slopes gently downwards towards the west and north, and slightly to the south and south-west.[\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-128) This plateau is surrounded by the [Great Escarpment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Escarpment,_Southern_Africa "Great Escarpment, Southern Africa")[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Altas-129) whose eastern, and highest, stretch is known as the [Drakensberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drakensberg "Drakensberg").[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-130) [Mafadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mafadi "Mafadi") in the Drakensberg is the highest peak in the country at 3,450m (11,320ft) above sea level. The KwaZulu-Natal–Lesotho international border is formed by the highest portion of the Great Escarpment which reaches an altitude of over 3,000m (9,800ft).[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-131)

The south and south-western parts of the plateau (at approximately 1,100–1,800m above sea level) and the adjoining plain below (at approximately 700–800m above sea level–see map on the right) are together known as the [Great Karoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karoo "Karoo"), which consists of sparsely populated [shrubland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrubland "Shrubland"). To the north, the Great Karoo fades into the more arid [Bushmanland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bushmanland,_Northern_Cape "Bushmanland, Northern Cape"), which eventually becomes the [Kalahari Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalahari_Desert "Kalahari Desert") in the north-west of the country. The mid-eastern and highest part of the plateau is known as the [Highveld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highveld "Highveld"). This relatively well-watered area is home to a great proportion of the country's commercial farmlands and contains its largest conurbation, [Gauteng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauteng#Demographics#Urban_conurbation "Gauteng"). To the north of the Highveld, from about the 25°30'S line of latitude, the plateau slopes downwards into the [Bushveld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bushveld "Bushveld"), which ultimately gives way to the [Limpopo River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limpopo_River "Limpopo River") lowlands, the [Lowveld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veld#Highveld_and_Lowveld "Veld").[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Altas-129)

The coastal belt, below the Great Escarpment, moving clockwise from the northeast, consists of the Limpopo Lowveld, which merges into the Mpumalanga Lowveld, below the Mpumalanga Drakensberg (the eastern portion of the Great Escarpment).[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-132) This is hotter, drier and less intensely cultivated than the Highveld above the escarpment.[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Altas-129) The [Kruger National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kruger_National_Park "Kruger National Park"), located in the provinces of Limpopo and Mpumalanga in north-eastern South Africa, occupies a large portion of the Lowveld, covering 19,633 square kilometres (7,580 sq mi)[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Kruger_National_Park-133)

[![Image depicting the Drakensberg](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/South_Africa_-_Drakensberg_%2816261357780%29.jpg/500px-South_Africa_-_Drakensberg_%2816261357780%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:South_Africa_-_Drakensberg_(16261357780).jpg) [Drakensberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drakensberg "Drakensberg"), the eastern and highest portion of the [Great Escarpment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Escarpment,_Southern_Africa "Great Escarpment, Southern Africa") which surrounds the east, south and western borders of the central plateau

The coastal belt below the south and south-western stretches of the Great Escarpment contains several ranges of [Cape Fold Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Fold_Belt "Cape Fold Belt") which run parallel to the coast, separating the Great Escarpment from the ocean.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-134)[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-geological_map-135) The land between the [Outeniqua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outeniqua_Mountains "Outeniqua Mountains") and [Langeberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Langeberg "Langeberg") ranges to the south and the [Swartberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swartberg "Swartberg") range to the north is known as the [Little Karoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karoo "Karoo"),[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Altas-129) which consists of semi-desert shrubland similar to that of the Great Karoo, except that its northern strip along the foothills of the Swartberg Mountains has a somewhat higher rainfall and is, therefore, more cultivated than the Great Karoo.

The Little Karoo is famous for its [ostrich farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_ostrich#Humans#Economic_use "Common ostrich") around [Oudtshoorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oudtshoorn "Oudtshoorn"). The lowland area to the north of the Swartberg range up to the Great Escarpment is the lowland part of the Great Karoo, which is climatically and botanically almost indistinguishable from the Karoo above the Great Escarpment. The narrow coastal strip between the Outeniqua and Langeberg ranges and the ocean, known as the [Garden Route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_Route "Garden Route"), has a moderately high year-round rainfall, and hosts the [most extensive area of forest in the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knysna%E2%80%93Amatole_montane_forests "Knysna–Amatole montane forests").

In the south-west corner of the country, the [Cape Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Peninsula "Cape Peninsula") forms the southernmost tip of the coastal strip which borders the Atlantic Ocean and ultimately terminates at the country's border with Namibia at the [Orange River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_River "Orange River"). The Cape Peninsula has a [Mediterranean climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_climate "Mediterranean climate"), making it and its immediate surrounds the only portion of [Sub-Saharan Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-Saharan_Africa "Sub-Saharan Africa") which receives most of its rainfall in winter.[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-136)[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Altas1-137)

The coastal belt to the north of the Cape Peninsula is bounded on the west by the Atlantic Ocean and the first row of north–south running Cape Fold Mountains to the east. The Cape Fold Mountains peter out at about the 32°S line of latitude,[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-geological_map-135) after which the Great Escarpment bounds the coastal plain. The most southerly portion of this coastal belt is known as the [Swartland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swartland "Swartland") and Malmesbury Plain, which is an important wheat-growing region, relying on winter rains. The region further north is known as [Namaqualand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namaqualand "Namaqualand"),[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-138) which becomes more arid near the Orange River. The little rain that falls tends to fall in winter,[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Altas1-137) which results in one of the world's most spectacular displays of flowers carpeting huge stretches of [veld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veld "Veld") in spring (August–September).

South Africa also has one offshore possession, the small [sub-Antarctic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subantarctic "Subantarctic") archipelago of the Prince Edward Islands, consisting of [Marion Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marion_Island "Marion Island") (290km2 or 110sqmi) and [Prince Edward Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Edward_Islands "Prince Edward Islands") (45km2 or 17sqmi).

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_South_Africa "Climate of South Africa")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/South_Africa_K%C3%B6ppen.svg/500px-South_Africa_K%C3%B6ppen.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:South_Africa_K%C3%B6ppen.svg) [Köppen climate types](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification "Köppen climate classification") of South Africa

South Africa has a generally [temperate climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_climate "Temperate climate") because it is surrounded by the Atlantic and Indian Oceans on three sides, because it is located in the climatically milder [Southern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Hemisphere "Southern Hemisphere"), and because its average elevation rises steadily toward the north (toward the equator) and further inland. This varied topography and oceanic influence result in a great variety of climatic zones. The climatic zones range from the extreme desert of the southern [Namib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namib "Namib") in the farthest northwest to the lush subtropical climate in the east along the border with Mozambique and the Indian Ocean. Winters in South Africa occur between June and August. The extreme southwest has a climate similar to that of the [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_climate "Mediterranean climate") with wet winters and hot, dry summers, hosting the famous [fynbos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fynbos "Fynbos") [biome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biome "Biome") of shrubland and [thicket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albany_thickets "Albany thickets"). This area produces much of the wine in South Africa and is known for its wind, which blows intermittently almost all year. The severity of this wind made passing around the Cape of Good Hope particularly treacherous for sailors, causing many shipwrecks. Further east on the south coast, rainfall is distributed more evenly throughout the year, producing a green landscape. The annual rainfall increases south of the Lowveld, especially near the coast, which is [subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subtropics "Subtropics"). The Free State is particularly flat because it lies centrally on the high plateau. North of the [Vaal River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaal_River "Vaal River"), the Highveld becomes better watered and does not experience subtropical extremes of heat. Johannesburg, in the centre of the Highveld, is at 1,740m (5,709ft) above sea level and receives an annual rainfall of 760mm (29.9in). Winters in this region are cold, although snow is rare.[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-139)

The coldest place on mainland South Africa is Buffelsfontein in the [Eastern Cape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Cape "Eastern Cape"), where a temperature of −20.1°C (−4.2°F) was recorded in 2013.[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-140) The Prince Edward Islands have colder average annual temperatures, but Buffelsfontein has colder extremes. The deep interior of mainland South Africa has the hottest temperatures: a temperature of 51.7°C (125.06°F) was recorded in 1948 in the Northern Cape Kalahari near [Upington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upington "Upington"),[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-141) but this temperature is unofficial and was not recorded with standard equipment; the official highest temperature is 48.8°C (119.84°F) at [Vioolsdrif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vioolsdrif "Vioolsdrif") in January 1993.[\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-142)

[Climate change in South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_South_Africa "Climate change in South Africa") is leading to increased temperatures and rainfall variability. [Extreme weather](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_weather "Extreme weather") events are becoming more prominent.[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-143) This is a critical concern for South Africans as climate change will affect the overall status and wellbeing of the country, for example with regards to [water resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_resources "Water resources"). Speedy environmental changes are resulting in clear effects on the community and environmental level in different ways and aspects, starting with air quality, to temperature and weather patterns, reaching out to food security and disease burden.[\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-144) According to computer-generated climate modelling produced by the South African National Biodiversity Institute,[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-SANBI-145) parts of southern Africa will see an increase in temperature by about 1°C (1.8°F) along the coast to more than 4°C (7.2°F) in the already hot [hinterland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinterland "Hinterland") such as the Northern Cape in late spring and summertime by 2050. The [Cape Floral Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Floristic_Region "Cape Floristic Region") is predicted to be hit very hard by climate change. Drought, increased intensity and frequency of fire, and climbing temperatures are expected to push many rare species towards extinction. South Africa has published two national climate change reports in 2011 and 2016.[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-146) South Africa contributes considerable [carbon dioxide emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_gas_emissions "Greenhouse gas emissions"), being the 14th largest emitter of carbon dioxide,[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Carbon_Brief-2018-147) primarily from its heavy reliance on coal and oil for [energy production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_development "Energy development").[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Carbon_Brief-2018-147) As part of its international commitments, South Africa has pledged to peak emissions between 2020 and 2025.[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Carbon_Brief-2018-147)

### Biodiversity

Main article: [Biodiversity of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_of_South_Africa "Biodiversity of South Africa")

See also: [Wildlife of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_South_Africa "Wildlife of South Africa"), [Protected areas of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_areas_of_South_Africa "Protected areas of South Africa"), and [Marine biodiversity of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_biodiversity_of_South_Africa "Marine biodiversity of South Africa")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Antidorcas_marsupialis%2C_male_%28Etosha%2C_2012%29.jpg/500px-Antidorcas_marsupialis%2C_male_%28Etosha%2C_2012%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Antidorcas_marsupialis,_male_(Etosha,_2012).jpg) The [national animal of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_symbols_of_South_Africa "National symbols of South Africa") is the [springbok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Springbok "Springbok").
