### Languages

Main article: [Languages of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_South_Africa "Languages of South Africa")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/South_Africa_2011_dominant_language_map_%28hex_cells%29.svg/500px-South_Africa_2011_dominant_language_map_%28hex_cells%29.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:South_Africa_2011_dominant_language_map_(hex_cells).svg)

Map showing the dominant [South African languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_South_Africa "Languages of South Africa") by area

- [Zulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulu_language "Zulu language") (24.4%)

- [Xhosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xhosa_language "Xhosa language") (16.0%)

- ⁠ [Afrikaans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrikaans "Afrikaans") (13.5%)

- [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_English "South African English") (9.6%)

- [Pedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedi_language "Pedi language") (9.1%)

- [Tswana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tswana_language "Tswana language") (8.0%)

- [Southern Sotho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sotho_language "Sotho language") (7.6%)

- [Tsonga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsonga_language "Tsonga language") (4.5%)

- [Swazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swazi_language "Swazi language") (2.5%)

- [Venda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venda_language "Venda language") (2.4%)

- [Southern Ndebele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Ndebele_language "Southern Ndebele language") (2.1%)

- None dominant

- Areas of little or no population


South Africa has 12 official languages:[\[6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-parliament.gov.za-9) [Afrikaans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrikaans "Afrikaans"), [Zulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulu_language "Zulu language"), [Xhosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xhosa_language "Xhosa language"), [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_English "South African English"), [Pedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Sotho "Northern Sotho"),[\[316\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-320) [Tswana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tswana_language "Tswana language"), [Southern Sotho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sotho_language "Sotho language"), [Tsonga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsonga_language "Tsonga language"), [Swazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swazi_language "Swazi language"), [Venda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venda_language "Venda language"), and [Southern Ndebele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Ndebele_language "Southern Ndebele language") (in order of first language speakers), as well as South African Sign Language, which was recognised as an official language in 2023.[\[6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-parliament.gov.za-9) In this regard, it is fourth only to [Bolivia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivia "Bolivia"), [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_languages_of_India "Official languages of India"), and [Zimbabwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zimbabwe "Zimbabwe") in number. While all the languages are formally equal, some languages are spoken more than others. According to the 2022 census, the three most spoken first languages are Zulu (24.4%), Xhosa (16.6%), and Afrikaans (10.6%).[\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Census-2022-11) Although English is recognised as the language of commerce and science, it is only the fifth most common home language, that of only 8.7% of South Africans in 2022; nevertheless, it has become the de facto [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca "Lingua franca") of the nation.[\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Census-2022-11) Estimates based on the 1991 census suggest just under half of South Africans could speak English.[\[317\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-321) It is the second most commonly spoken language outside of the household, after Zulu.[\[318\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-322)

Other languages are spoken, or were widely used previously, including [Fanagalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fanagalo_language "Fanagalo language"), [Khoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khoe_language "Khoe language"), [Lobedu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobedu_language "Lobedu language"), [Nama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nama_language "Nama language"), [Northern Ndebele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ndebele_language "Northern Ndebele language"), and [Phuthi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phuthi_language "Phuthi language").[\[319\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-323) Many of the unofficial [languages of the San and Khoekhoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khoisan_languages "Khoisan languages") peoples contain regional dialects stretching northwards into Namibia and Botswana, and elsewhere. These people, who are a physically distinct population from the Bantu people who make up most of the Black Africans in South Africa, have their own cultural identity based on their [hunter-gatherer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer "Hunter-gatherer") societies. They have been marginalised, and the remainder of their languages are in danger of becoming [extinct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extinct_language "Extinct language").[\[320\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-324)

White South Africans may also speak European languages, including Italian, Portuguese (also spoken by black Angolans and Mozambicans), Dutch, German, and Greek, while some Indian South Africans and more recent migrants from South Asia speak [Indian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_India "Languages of India"), such as Gujarati, Hindi, Tamil, Telugu, and Urdu. French is spoken by migrants from [Francophone Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_French "African French").

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_South_Africa "Religion in South Africa")

Religion in South Africa (2022 census[\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Census-2022-11))

1. [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity "Christianity") (85.3%)
2. [Traditional faiths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_African_religions "Traditional African religions") (7.80%)
3. [No religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion "Irreligion") (3.10%)
4. [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam "Islam") (1.60%)
5. [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism "Hinduism") (1.10%)
6. Others (1.10%)

According to the 2022 census, Christians accounted for 85.3% of the population, with a majority of them being members of various [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism "Protestantism") [denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denomination "Christian denomination") (broadly defined to include [syncretic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism "Syncretism") [African-initiated churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-initiated_church "African-initiated church")) and a minority of [Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church "Catholic Church") and other Christians. Per the 2001 census, the Christian category included [Zion Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zion_Christian_Church "Zion Christian Church") (11.1%), [Pentecostal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostalism "Pentecostalism") ( [Charismatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charismatic_movement "Charismatic movement")) (8.2%), [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_South_Africa "Catholic Church in South Africa") (7.1%), [Methodist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodist_Church_of_Southern_Africa "Methodist Church of Southern Africa") (6.8%), [Dutch Reformed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Reformed_Church_in_South_Africa_(NGK) "Dutch Reformed Church in South Africa (NGK)") (6.7%), and [Anglican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Church_of_Southern_Africa "Anglican Church of Southern Africa") (3.8%). Members of the remaining Christian churches accounted for the rest of the Christian population. Per the 2022 census, [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_South_Africa "Islam in South Africa") accounted for 1.6% of the population, [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_South_Africa "Hinduism in South Africa") 1.1%, [traditional African religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_African_religions "Traditional African religions") 7.8%, 3.1% had no religious affiliation, and 1.1% were "other".[\[226\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-factbook2222-230)[\[321\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-state.gov-325)[\[322\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-326)[\[323\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-327)

African-initiated churches formed the largest of the Christian groups. It was believed that many of the persons who claimed no affiliation with any organised religion adhered to a traditional African religion. There are an estimated 200,000 [traditional healers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_healers_of_Southern_Africa "Traditional healers of Southern Africa"), and up to 60% of South Africans consult these healers,[\[324\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-328) generally called _sangoma_ ('diviner') or _inyanga_ ('herbalist'). These healers use a combination of [ancestral spiritual beliefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veneration_of_the_dead "Veneration of the dead") and a belief in the spiritual and medicinal properties of local fauna, flora, and funga commonly known as [_muti_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_African_medicine "Traditional African medicine") ('medicine'), to facilitate healing in clients. Many peoples have syncretic religious practices combining Christian and indigenous influences.[\[325\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-DoS-329)

South African Muslims comprise mainly Coloureds and Indians. They have been joined by black or white South African converts as well as those from other parts of Africa.[\[326\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-csmonitor-330) South African Muslims describe their faith as the fastest-growing religion of conversion in the country, with the number of black Muslims growing sixfold, from 12,000 in 1991 to 74,700 in 2004.[\[326\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-csmonitor-330)[\[327\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-331)

There is a substantial [Jewish population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_South_Africa "History of the Jews in South Africa"), descended from [European Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Europe "History of the Jews in Europe") who arrived as a minority amongst other European settlers. This population peaked in the 1970s at 118,000, though only around 75,000 remain today, the rest having emigrated, mostly to Israel.[\[328\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-332) The present-day Jewish community in South Africa is the largest on the African continent, and the twelfth-largest in the world.

### Education

Main article: [Education in South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_South_Africa "Education in South Africa")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/UCT_Upper_Campus_landscape_view.jpg/500px-UCT_Upper_Campus_landscape_view.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:UCT_Upper_Campus_landscape_view.jpg) The [University of Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Cape_Town "University of Cape Town")

The adult [literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy "Literacy") rate in 2025 was 95%. This was the second-highest in [Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Africa "Africa"), behind only Seychelles.[\[329\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-lit01-333) South Africa has a [three-tier system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three-tier_education "Three-tier education") of education starting with primary school, followed by high school, and tertiary education in the form of (academic) universities and universities of technology. Learners have twelve years of formal schooling, from grade 1 to 12. Grade R, or grade 0, is a pre-primary foundation year.[\[330\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-edufacts0831-334) Primary schools span the first seven years of schooling.[\[331\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-335) High school education spans a further five years. The [National Senior Certificate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matriculation_in_South_Africa "Matriculation in South Africa") examination takes place at the end of grade 12, and is necessary for tertiary studies at a [South African university](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_South_Africa "List of universities in South Africa").[\[330\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-edufacts0831-334) Public universities are divided into three types: traditional universities, which offer theoretically oriented university degrees; [universities of technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_technology "Institute of technology") (formerly called _[technikons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technikon "Technikon")_), which offer vocationally-oriented diplomas and degrees; and comprehensive universities, which offer both types of qualification. There are 23 public universities in South Africa: 11 traditional universities, 6 universities of technology, and 6 comprehensive universities. There are also a large amount of FET (Further Education and Training) and [TVET](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocational_education "Vocational education") (Technical and Vocational Education and Training) [colleges in South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_public_colleges_in_South_Africa "List of public colleges in South Africa").[\[332\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-336)[\[333\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-337)[\[334\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-338)

Under apartheid, schools for black people were subject to discrimination through inadequate funding and a separate syllabus called _[Bantu Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_Education_Act,_1953 "Bantu Education Act, 1953")_ which only taught skills sufficient to work as labourers.[\[335\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-339)

In 2004, South Africa started reforming its tertiary education system, merging and incorporating small universities into larger institutions, and renaming all tertiary education institutions "university". By 2015, 1.4million students in higher education have been aided by a financial aid scheme which was promulgated in 1999.[\[336\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Cele-340)

### Health

Main articles: [Health in South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_South_Africa "Health in South Africa") and [Healthcare in South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_South_Africa "Healthcare in South Africa")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/Groote_Schuur_Hospital%2C_Observatory%2C_Cape_Town%2C_Western_Cape._05.JPG/500px-Groote_Schuur_Hospital%2C_Observatory%2C_Cape_Town%2C_Western_Cape._05.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Groote_Schuur_Hospital,_Observatory,_Cape_Town,_Western_Cape._05.JPG) [Groote Schuur Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groote_Schuur_Hospital "Groote Schuur Hospital") in [Observatory, Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Observatory,_Cape_Town "Observatory, Cape Town"), site of the world's first successful human-to-human [heart transplant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heart_transplantation "Heart transplantation")

According to the [South African Institute of Race Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_Institute_of_Race_Relations "South African Institute of Race Relations"), the life expectancy in 2009 was 71 years for a white South African and 48 years for a black South African.[\[337\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-341) The healthcare spending in the country is about 9% of GDP.[\[338\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-smartpl-342) About 84% of the population depends on the public healthcare system,[\[338\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-smartpl-342) which is beset with chronic human resource shortages and limited resources.[\[339\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-icap-343) About 20% of the population use private healthcare.[\[340\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-fmhealth-344) Only 16% of the population are covered by [medical aid schemes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance "Health insurance");[\[341\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-medicl-345) the rest pay for private care [out-of-pocket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Out-of-pocket_expense "Out-of-pocket expense") or through in-hospital-only plans.[\[340\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-fmhealth-344) The three dominant hospital groups, [Mediclinic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediclinic_International "Mediclinic International"), [Life Healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LIFE_Healthcare_Group "LIFE Healthcare Group") and [Netcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netcare "Netcare"), together control 75% of the private hospital market.[\[340\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-fmhealth-344) These disparities have driven ongoing policy efforts to reform the healthcare system, including the introduction of [National Health Insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Insurance_Act,_2023 "National Health Insurance Act, 2023") (NHI) aimed at expanding equitable access to healthcare.[\[342\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-346)

#### HIV/AIDS

Main article: [HIV/AIDS in South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS_in_South_Africa "HIV/AIDS in South Africa")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Life_expectancy_in_select_African_countries%2C_1950%E2%80%932019.jpg/500px-Life_expectancy_in_select_African_countries%2C_1950%E2%80%932019.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Life_expectancy_in_select_African_countries,_1950%E2%80%932019.jpg) Life expectancy in select Southern African countries, 1950–2019. [HIV/AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS "HIV/AIDS") has caused a fall in life expectancy.

According to the 2015 [UNAIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_United_Nations_Programme_on_HIV/AIDS "Joint United Nations Programme on HIV/AIDS") medical report, South Africa has an estimated seven million people who are living with HIV– more than any other country in the world.[\[343\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-347) In 2018, HIV prevalence—the percentage of people living with HIV—among adults (15–49 years) was 20.4%, and in the same year 71,000 people died from an AIDS-related illness.[\[344\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-348)

A 2008 study revealed that HIV/AIDS infection is distinctly divided along racial lines: 13.6% of blacks are HIV-positive, whereas only 0.3% of whites have the virus.[\[345\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-ten-349) Most deaths are experienced by economically active individuals, resulting in many [AIDS orphans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIDS_orphan "AIDS orphan") who, in many cases, depend on the state for care and financial support.[\[346\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-avertaids-350) It is estimated that there are 1,200,000 orphans in South Africa.[\[346\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-avertaids-350)

The link between HIV, a virus spread primarily by sexual contact, and AIDS was long [denied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS_denialism "HIV/AIDS denialism") by President [Thabo Mbeki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thabo_Mbeki "Thabo Mbeki") and his health minister, [Manto Tshabalala-Msimang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manto_Tshabalala-Msimang "Manto Tshabalala-Msimang"), who insisted that the many deaths in the country are caused by [malnutrition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malnutrition_in_South_Africa "Malnutrition in South Africa"), and hence poverty, and not HIV.[\[347\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-351) In 2007, in response to international pressure, the government made efforts to fight AIDS.[\[348\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-www.info.gov.za-352) After the [2009 general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_South_African_general_election "2009 South African general election"), President Jacob Zuma appointed [Aaron Motsoaledi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaron_Motsoaledi "Aaron Motsoaledi") as the health minister and committed his government to increasing funding for and widening the scope of HIV treatment,[\[349\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-353) and by 2015, South Africa had made significant progress, with the widespread availability of [antiretroviral drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Management_of_HIV/AIDS "Management of HIV/AIDS") resulted in an increase in life expectancy from 52.1 years to 62.5 years.[\[350\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-conv1-354)

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_South_Africa "Culture of South Africa")

The South African black majority still has a substantial number of rural inhabitants who lead largely impoverished lives. It is among these people that cultural traditions survive most strongly; as black people have become increasingly urbanised and [Westernised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_world "Western world"), aspects of traditional culture have declined. Members of the middle class, who have historically been predominantly white but whose ranks include growing numbers of black, Coloured and Indian people,[\[351\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-355)[\[352\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-356) have lifestyles similar in many respects to that of people found in [Western Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Europe "Western Europe"), [North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_America "North America") and [Australasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australasia "Australasia").

### Arts

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/San_Rock_Art_-_Cederberg.jpg/500px-San_Rock_Art_-_Cederberg.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:San_Rock_Art_-_Cederberg.jpg) [Rock painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_art "Rock art") by the [San people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_people "San people"), [Cederberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cederberg "Cederberg")

[South African art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_art "South African art") includes the oldest art objects in the world, which were discovered in a South African cave and dated from roughly 75,000 years ago.[\[353\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-357) The scattered tribes of the Khoisan peoples moving into South Africa from around 10,000 BC had their own fluent art styles seen today in a multitude of cave paintings. They were superseded by the Bantu/Nguni peoples with their own vocabularies of art forms. Forms of art evolved in the mines and townships: a dynamic art using everything from plastic strips to bicycle spokes. The Dutch-influenced folk art of the Afrikaner [_trekboers_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trekboer "Trekboer") and the urban white artists, earnestly following changing European traditions from the 1850s onwards, also contributed to this eclectic mix which continues to evolve to this day.

### Media

Main article: [Mass media in South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_South_Africa "Mass media in South Africa")

The South African media sector is large, and South Africa is one of Africa's major media centres. While the many broadcasters and publications reflect the diversity of the population as a whole, the most commonly used language is English. However, all ten other official languages are represented to some extent. South Africa's media landscape is diverse and well-developed, comprising the public broadcaster [SABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SABC "SABC"), the private [free-to-air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free-to-air "Free-to-air") channel [E.tv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E.tv "E.tv"), and [satellite television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_television "Satellite television") provider [MultiChoice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MultiChoice "MultiChoice"), which operates [DStv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DStv "DStv") across sub-Saharan Africa. The country has partially rolled out [digital terrestrial television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_terrestrial_television "Digital terrestrial television") (DTT), though full migration from analogue broadcasting has experienced delays.[\[354\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-358)

### Popular culture

Further information: [Music of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_South_Africa "Music of South Africa")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/2014-11-26_traditional_Zulu_performance_02_anagoria.JPG/500px-2014-11-26_traditional_Zulu_performance_02_anagoria.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:2014-11-26_traditional_Zulu_performance_02_anagoria.JPG) [Zulus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulus "Zulus") perform a traditional dance.

There is great diversity in [South African music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_South_Africa "Music of South Africa"). Black musicians have developed styles called [Kwaito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kwaito "Kwaito") and [Amapiano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amapiano "Amapiano").[\[355\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-359) Of note is [Brenda Fassie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brenda_Fassie "Brenda Fassie"), who launched to fame with her song " [Weekend Special](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weekend_Special "Weekend Special")", which was sung in English. More famous traditional musicians include [Ladysmith Black Mambazo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladysmith_Black_Mambazo "Ladysmith Black Mambazo"), while the [Soweto String Quartet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soweto_String_Quartet "Soweto String Quartet") performs classical music with an African flavour. South Africa has produced world-famous jazz musicians, notably [Hugh Masekela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugh_Masekela "Hugh Masekela"), [Jonas Gwangwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonas_Gwangwa "Jonas Gwangwa"), [Abdullah Ibrahim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_Ibrahim "Abdullah Ibrahim"), [Miriam Makeba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miriam_Makeba "Miriam Makeba"), [Jonathan Butler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Butler "Jonathan Butler"), [Chris McGregor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_McGregor "Chris McGregor"), and [Sathima Bea Benjamin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sathima_Bea_Benjamin "Sathima Bea Benjamin").

Afrikaans music covers multiple genres, such as the contemporary [Steve Hofmeyr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Hofmeyr "Steve Hofmeyr"), the [punk rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punk_rock "Punk rock") band [Fokofpolisiekar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fokofpolisiekar "Fokofpolisiekar"), and the singer-songwriter [Jeremy Loops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremy_Loops "Jeremy Loops"). South African popular musicians that have found international success include [Manfred Mann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manfred_Mann_(musician) "Manfred Mann (musician)"), [Johnny Clegg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnny_Clegg "Johnny Clegg"), rap-rave duo [Die Antwoord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Die_Antwoord "Die Antwoord"), [Tyla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyla_(South_African_singer) "Tyla (South African singer)"), and rock band [Seether](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seether "Seether"). Rappers such as [AKA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AKA_(rapper) "AKA (rapper)"), [Nasty C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasty_C "Nasty C") and [Cassper Nyovest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassper_Nyovest "Cassper Nyovest") gained notoriety in other avenues like the BET Awards for best African acts.

Although few [South African film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_South_Africa "Cinema of South Africa") productions are known outside South Africa, many foreign films have been produced about South Africa. Arguably, the most high-profile film portraying South Africa in recent years was _[District 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_9 "District 9")_, as well as _[Chappie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chappie_(film) "Chappie (film)")_. Other notable exceptions are the film _[Tsotsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsotsi "Tsotsi")_, which won the [Academy Award for Foreign Language Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_International_Feature_Film "Academy Award for Best International Feature Film") at the [78th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/78th_Academy_Awards "78th Academy Awards") in 2006, as well as _[U-Carmen e-Khayelitsha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U-Carmen_e-Khayelitsha "U-Carmen e-Khayelitsha")_, which won the [Golden Bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Bear "Golden Bear") at the 2005 [Berlin International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_International_Film_Festival "Berlin International Film Festival"). In 2015, the [Oliver Hermanus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_Hermanus "Oliver Hermanus") film [_The Endless River_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Endless_River_(film) "The Endless River (film)") became the first South African film selected for the [Venice Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venice_Film_Festival "Venice Film Festival").

### Literature

Main article: [South African literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_literature "South African literature")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/83/Sol_Plaatje_002.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Sol_Plaatje_002.jpg)

[Sol Plaatje](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sol_Plaatje "Sol Plaatje")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Alan_Paton.jpg/250px-Alan_Paton.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Alan_Paton.jpg)

[Alan Paton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Paton "Alan Paton")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/1/1e/Archibald_Campbell_Mzolisa_Jordan_.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Archibald_Campbell_Mzolisa_Jordan_.jpg)

[Archibald Campbell Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archibald_Campbell_Jordan "Archibald Campbell Jordan")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Breyten_Breytenbach%2C_Bestanddeelnr_932-4729.jpg/250px-Breyten_Breytenbach%2C_Bestanddeelnr_932-4729.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Breyten_Breytenbach,_Bestanddeelnr_932-4729.jpg)

[Breyten Breytenbach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breyten_Breytenbach "Breyten Breytenbach")

[South African literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_literature "South African literature") emerged from a unique social and political history. It "embraces all the languages and cultures of the people of South Africa" and began as an oral tradition.[\[356\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-360)[\[357\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-361) South Africa has 11 official spoken languages: Sepedi, Sesotho, Setswana, siSwati, Tshivenda, Xitsonga, Afrikaans, English, isiNdebele, isiXhosa and isiZulu.[\[358\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-362)

#### Literature in English

Notable white South African authors include anti-apartheid activist [Alan Paton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Paton "Alan Paton"), who published the novel _[Cry, the Beloved Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cry,_the_Beloved_Country "Cry, the Beloved Country")_ in 1948. [Nadine Gordimer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nadine_Gordimer "Nadine Gordimer") became the first South African to be awarded the [Nobel Prize in Literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobel_Prize_in_Literature "Nobel Prize in Literature"), in 1991. [J.M. Coetzee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._M._Coetzee "J. M. Coetzee") won the Nobel Prize in Literature in 2003. When awarding the prize, the [Swedish Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_Academy "Swedish Academy") stated that Coetzee "in innumerable guises portrays the surprising involvement of the outsider."[\[359\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Swedish_Academy-363)

[Antjie Krog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antjie_Krog "Antjie Krog") is a poet, journalist, academic and writer noted for confronting apartheid and its aftermath in her work. She reported on the Truth and Reconciliation Commission for the South African Broadcasting Corporation, and her best-known book is _[Country of My Skull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_of_My_Skull "Country of My Skull")_ (1998).[\[360\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-364) Elsa Joubert was an Afrikaans novelist, travel-writer and journalist whose work draws on African landscapes and human experiences. She is best known for the 1979 novel _[Die swerfjare van Poppie Nongena (The Long Journey of Poppie Nongena)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Long_Journey_of_Poppie_Nongena "The Long Journey of Poppie Nongena")_, a story of a Black South African woman's endurance under apartheid, translated into many languages and widely celebrated.[\[361\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-365)

One of the first well-known novels written by a black author in an African language was [Solomon Thekiso Plaatje](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sol_Plaatje "Sol Plaatje")'s _[Mhudi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mhudi "Mhudi")_, written in 1930. During the 1950s, _[Drum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drum_(South_African_magazine) "Drum (South African magazine)")_ magazine became a hotbed of political satire, fiction, and essays, giving a voice to the urban black culture.

The plays of [Athol Fugard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athol_Fugard "Athol Fugard") have been regularly premiered in [fringe theatres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fringe_theatre "Fringe theatre") in South Africa, London ( [Royal Court Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Court_Theatre "Royal Court Theatre")) and New York. [Olive Schreiner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive_Schreiner "Olive Schreiner")'s _[The Story of an African Farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Story_of_an_African_Farm "The Story of an African Farm")_ (1883) was a revelation in [Victorian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_literature "Victorian literature"): it has become recognised as one of the first feminist novels.[\[362\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-366)

[Breyten Breytenbach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breyten_Breytenbach "Breyten Breytenbach") was jailed for his involvement with the guerrilla movement against apartheid.[\[363\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-367) [André Brink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Brink "André Brink") was the first Afrikaner writer to be [banned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banned_book "Banned book") by the government after he released the novel _[A Dry White Season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Dry_White_Season_(novel) "A Dry White Season (novel)")_.[\[364\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-368)

### Cuisine

Main article: [South African cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_cuisine "South African cuisine")

See also: [South African wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_wine "South African wine")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Melktert.jpg/500px-Melktert.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Melktert.jpg) [Milk tart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melktert "Melktert"), a traditional South African dessert made with a creamy custard filling

[South African cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_cuisine "South African cuisine") is diverse and reflects the country's multicultural heritage, incorporating influences from indigenous African, [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Dutch "Cape Dutch"), [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_cuisine "British cuisine"), [Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_South_Africans "Indian South Africans") and [Cape Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Malays "Cape Malays") culinary traditions. Meat plays a central role in many regional food cultures, most notably through the _[braai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Braai "Braai")_, an originally [Afrikaner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrikaners "Afrikaners") variation of the [barbecue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbecue "Barbecue") that functions as both a cooking method and a widely shared social custom across communities. Common braai staples include _[boerewors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boerewors "Boerewors")_ (spiced sausage), [lamb chops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamb_and_mutton "Lamb and mutton"), [steak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steak "Steak"), [_pap_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mielie_meal "Mielie meal") (maize porridge) and _[chakalaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chakalaka "Chakalaka")_ (spicy relish). Traditional dishes also vary by region and heritage, including _[bobotie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobotie "Bobotie")_, a curried minced meat dish with an egg-based topping; _[bunny chow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bunny_chow "Bunny chow")_, a hollowed-out loaf of bread filled with [curry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curry "Curry") originating in Durban; and _[potjiekos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potjiekos "Potjiekos")_, a slow-cooked stew prepared in a cast-iron pot over an open flame. Street foods such as _[vetkoek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vetkoek "Vetkoek")_, [_gatsby_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gatsby_(sandwich) "Gatsby (sandwich)") sandwiches, [_samoosas_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samosa "Samosa") and _[biltong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biltong "Biltong")_ (air-dried cured meat) are widely consumed, while popular desserts such as [_milk tart_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melktert "Melktert") and _[koeksisters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koeksister "Koeksister")_ reflect the country's layered culinary traditions.[\[365\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-369)

South Africa also has a highly developed [fast-food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast_food "Fast food") culture, with several locally founded chains achieving national and international prominence. The most notable is [Nando's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nando's "Nando's"), founded in Johannesburg in 1987, which specialises in flame-grilled [peri-peri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piri_piri "Piri piri") chicken and operates more than 1,250 outlets in over 23 countries.[\[366\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-NandosGlobal2-370) Other major South African fast-food franchises include [Wimpy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wimpy_(restaurant) "Wimpy (restaurant)"), [Steers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steers_(restaurant) "Steers (restaurant)"), [Debonairs Pizza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debonairs_Pizza "Debonairs Pizza") and [Chicken Licken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicken_Licken_(restaurant) "Chicken Licken (restaurant)"), many of which have expanded throughout Africa and other regions. International fast-food brands are also well established in South Africa; the country is among the world's leading markets for [KFC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KFC "KFC") outlets, reflecting the widespread popularity of fast food across income groups and urban centres.[\[367\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-kfcstores2-371)

In the beverage sector, South Africa has played an influential role in both global [energy drinks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_drink "Energy drink") and [wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wine "Wine"). [Monster Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monster_Energy "Monster Energy"), although marketed as an American brand, was founded by South African-born entrepreneurs [Rodney Sacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodney_Sacks "Rodney Sacks") and [Hilton Schlosberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hilton_Schlosberg "Hilton Schlosberg"), who relocated to the United States and played a central role in the company's international expansion.[\[368\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-372) [South African wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_wine "South African wine") is also renowned internationally, with [vineyards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vineyard "Vineyard") in regions such as [Stellenbosch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellenbosch "Stellenbosch"), [Franschhoek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franschhoek "Franschhoek"), [Paarl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paarl "Paarl") and [Barrydale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barrydale "Barrydale"). These wine-producing areas form a significant part of the country's culinary tourism industry, contributing to South Africa's international reputation for food and drink.[\[369\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-373)

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_South_Africa "Sport in South Africa")

[![Aerial view of the Cape Town Stadium in Cape Town, South Africa](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/d/d5/South_Africa_-_Cape_Town_Drieankerbaai_from_Lion%27s_head.jpg/500px-South_Africa_-_Cape_Town_Drieankerbaai_from_Lion%27s_head.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:South_Africa_-_Cape_Town_Drieankerbaai_from_Lion's_head.jpg) [Cape Town Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Town_Stadium "Cape Town Stadium") is the 5th-largest stadium in South Africa, with a capacity of 55,000.

Sport plays a significant role in South African culture, and the country's most popular sports are [soccer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_football "Association football"), [rugby union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_union "Rugby union") and [cricket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cricket "Cricket").[\[370\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-374) Other sports with notable support are [swimming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swimming_(sport) "Swimming (sport)"), [athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_of_athletics "Sport of athletics"), [golf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golf "Golf"), [boxing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boxing "Boxing"), mixed [martial arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_arts "Martial arts"), [tennis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennis "Tennis"), [ringball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ringball "Ringball"), [field hockey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_hockey "Field hockey"), [surfing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surfing "Surfing") and [netball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netball "Netball").[\[371\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-375)

Soccer is the most popular sport in South Africa.[\[372\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-376)[\[373\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-377)[\[374\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-378) South Africa hosted the [2010 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_FIFA_World_Cup "2010 FIFA World Cup").[\[375\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-379) It hosted the [1996 African Cup of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_African_Cup_of_Nations "1996 African Cup of Nations"), with the national team [Bafana Bafana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa_national_soccer_team "South Africa national soccer team") going on to win the tournament. South Africa's men's U-20 team also won the [2025 U-20 Africa Cup of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_U-20_Africa_Cup_of_Nations "2025 U-20 Africa Cup of Nations"). In 2022, the [women's team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa_women's_national_soccer_team "South Africa women's national soccer team") also won the [Women's Africa Cup of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Women's_Africa_Cup_of_Nations "2022 Women's Africa Cup of Nations"), beating [Morocco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morocco_women's_national_football_team "Morocco women's national football team") 2–1 in [the final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Women's_Africa_Cup_of_Nations_Final "2022 Women's Africa Cup of Nations Final"). The women's team went on to reach the last 16 at the [2023 FIFA Women's World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_FIFA_Women's_World_Cup "2023 FIFA Women's World Cup"), beating [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italy_women's_national_football_team "Italy women's national football team") and tying with [Argentina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argentina_women's_national_football_team "Argentina women's national football team") in the group stage.

Famous [combat sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combat_sport "Combat sport") personalities include Baby Jake [Jacob Matlala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_Matlala "Jacob Matlala"), [Vuyani Bungu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vuyani_Bungu "Vuyani Bungu"), [Welcome Ncita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welcome_Ncita "Welcome Ncita"), [Dingaan Thobela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dingaan_Thobela "Dingaan Thobela"), [Corrie Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corrie_Sanders "Corrie Sanders"), [Gerrie Coetzee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerrie_Coetzee "Gerrie Coetzee"), [Brian Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Mitchell_(boxer) "Brian Mitchell (boxer)"), [Garreth McLellan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garreth_McLellan "Garreth McLellan") and current [UFC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultimate_Fighting_Championship "Ultimate Fighting Championship") Middleweight Champion [Dricus du Plessis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dricus_du_Plessis "Dricus du Plessis"). Durban surfer [Jordy Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordy_Smith "Jordy Smith") won the 2010 Billabong J-Bay Open making him the highest ranked surfer in the world. South Africa produced [Formula One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_One "Formula One") motor racing's 1979 world champion [Jody Scheckter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jody_Scheckter "Jody Scheckter"). Famous active [Grand Prix motorcycle racing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Prix_motorcycle_racing "Grand Prix motorcycle racing") personalities include [Brad Binder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brad_Binder "Brad Binder") and his younger brother [Darryn Binder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darryn_Binder "Darryn Binder").

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/Bokke_Webb_Ellis-beker_toer_JHB_20191107_145608.jpg/500px-Bokke_Webb_Ellis-beker_toer_JHB_20191107_145608.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bokke_Webb_Ellis-beker_toer_JHB_20191107_145608.jpg) The [Springboks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa_national_rugby_union_team "South Africa national rugby union team") on their tour of the country after winning the [2019 Rugby World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Rugby_World_Cup "2019 Rugby World Cup")

South Africa has won the [Rugby World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_World_Cup "Rugby World Cup") four times, the most wins of any country. South Africa first won the [1995 Rugby World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_Rugby_World_Cup "1995 Rugby World Cup"), which it hosted. They went on to win the tournament again in [2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Rugby_World_Cup "2007 Rugby World Cup"), [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Rugby_World_Cup "2019 Rugby World Cup") and [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Rugby_World_Cup "2023 Rugby World Cup").[\[376\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-380)

Cricket is one of the most played sports in South Africa. It has hosted the [2003 Cricket World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Cricket_World_Cup "2003 Cricket World Cup"), the [2007 World Twenty20 Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICC_World_Twenty20 "ICC World Twenty20"). South Africa's national cricket team, the [Proteas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa_national_cricket_team "South Africa national cricket team"), have also won the inaugural edition of the [1998 ICC KnockOut Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_ICC_KnockOut_Trophy "1998 ICC KnockOut Trophy") by defeating [West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Indies_national_cricket_team "West Indies national cricket team") in the final. The [2023 ICC Women's T20 World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_ICC_Women's_T20_World_Cup "2023 ICC Women's T20 World Cup") was hosted in South Africa and the [women's team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa_women's_national_cricket_team "South Africa women's national cricket team") won silver. The men's team won silver at the [2024 ICC T20 World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_ICC_Men's_T20_World_Cup "2024 ICC Men's T20 World Cup"), and won the [2023–2025 ICC World Test Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023%E2%80%932025_World_Test_Championship "2023–2025 World Test Championship"), beating Australia in the final. [South Africa's national blind cricket team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa_national_blind_cricket_team "South Africa national blind cricket team") also went on to win the inaugural edition of the [Blind Cricket World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Cricket_World_Cup "Blind Cricket World Cup") in 1998.[\[377\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-381)

In 2004, the swimming team of [Roland Schoeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roland_Schoeman "Roland Schoeman"), [Lyndon Ferns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_Ferns "Lyndon Ferns"), [Darian Townsend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darian_Townsend "Darian Townsend") and [Ryk Neethling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryk_Neethling "Ryk Neethling") won the gold medal at the [Olympic Games in Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Summer_Olympics "2004 Summer Olympics"), simultaneously breaking the world record in the 4×100 [Freestyle Relay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freestyle_Relay "Freestyle Relay"). [Penny Heyns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penny_Heyns "Penny Heyns") won Olympic Gold in the 1996 [Atlanta Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_Olympic_Games "Atlanta Olympic Games"), and more recently, swimmers [Tatjana Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatjana_Smith "Tatjana Smith") (née Schoenmaker), [Lara van Niekerk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lara_van_Niekerk "Lara van Niekerk"), [Akani Simbine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akani_Simbine "Akani Simbine") and [Wayde van Niekerk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayde_van_Niekerk "Wayde van Niekerk") have all broken records and won medals at both the Olympic and [Commonwealth Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Games "Commonwealth Games"), with Wayde van Niekerk being the world record holder in [400 metres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/400_metres "400 metres") since 2016. In 2012, [Oscar Pistorius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oscar_Pistorius "Oscar Pistorius") became the first double amputee sprinter to compete at the [Olympic Games in London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Summer_Olympics "2012 Summer Olympics"). [Gary Player](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Player "Gary Player") is regarded as one of the greatest golfers of all time, having won the [Career Grand Slam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Slam_(golf) "Grand Slam (golf)"), one of five to have done so.[\[378\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-382)

## Notes

1. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_ref-3)Pretoria serves as the executive capital of South Africa, hosting the Union Buildings and the offices of the President and Cabinet.[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-three_capitals1-2)
2. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_ref-5)Cape Town is the legislative capital, home to the Parliament of South Africa, including the National Assembly and the National Council of Provinces.[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-three_capitals1-2)
3. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_ref-6)Bloemfontein serves as the judicial capital, hosting the Supreme Court of Appeal, the highest court for non-constitutional matters in South Africa.[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-three_capitals1-2)
4. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_ref-19)[Cape Agulhas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Agulhas "Cape Agulhas") is the geographical southernmost point of the African continent, marking the southern extremity of the Republic of South Africa.[\[14\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-SANParks-17)[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_note-Britannica127-18)

310. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa#cite_ref-314)Lehohla, Pali (5 May 2005). ["Debate over race and censuses not peculiar to SA"](https://web.archive.org/web/20070814143522/http://www.statssa.gov.za/news_archive/05may2005_1.asp). _Business Report_. Archived from [the original](http://www.statssa.gov.za/news_archive/05may2005_1.asp) on 14 August 2007. Retrieved 25 August 2013. Others pointed out that the repeal of the Population Registration Act in 1991 removed any legal basis for specifying 'race'. The Identification Act of 1997 makes no mention of race. On the other hand, the Employment Equity Act speaks of 'designated groups' being 'black people, women and people with disabilities'. The Act defines 'black' as referring to 'Africans, coloureds and Indians'. Apartheid and the racial identification which underpinned it explicitly linked race with differential access to resources and power. If the post-apartheid order was committed to remedying this, race would have to be included in surveys and censuses, so that progress in eradicating the consequences of apartheid could be measured and monitored. This was the reasoning that led to a 'self-identifying' question about 'race' or 'population group' in both the 1996 and 2001 population censuses, and in Statistics SA's household survey programme.

## Further reading

- _A History of South Africa, Third Edition_. Leonard Thompson. [Yale University Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_University_Press "Yale University Press"). 2001\. 384 pages. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [0-300-08776-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-300-08776-4 "Special:BookSources/0-300-08776-4").
- _Economic Analysis and Policy Formulation for Post-Apartheid South Africa: Mission Report, Aug. 1991_. International Development Research Centre. IDRC Canada, 1991. vi, 46 p. Without ISBN.
- _Emerging Johannesburg: Perspectives on the Postapartheid City_. Richard Tomlinson, et al. 2003. 336 pages. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [0-415-93559-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-415-93559-8 "Special:BookSources/0-415-93559-8")
- _Making of Modern South Africa: Conquest, Segregation and Apartheid_. Nigel Worden. 2000. 194 pages. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [0-631-21661-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-631-21661-8 "Special:BookSources/0-631-21661-8").
- _South Africa: A Narrative History_. [Frank Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Welsh_(writer) "Frank Welsh (writer)"). Kodansha America. 1999. 606 pages. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [1-56836-258-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/1-56836-258-7 "Special:BookSources/1-56836-258-7")
- _South Africa in Contemporary Times_. [Godfrey Mwakikagile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Godfrey_Mwakikagile "Godfrey Mwakikagile"). New Africa Press. 2008. 260 pages. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [978-0-9802587-3-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-9802587-3-8 "Special:BookSources/978-0-9802587-3-8").
- _The Atlas of Changing South Africa_. A. J. Christopher. 2000. 216 pages. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [0-415-21178-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-415-21178-6 "Special:BookSources/0-415-21178-6").
- _The Politics of the New South Africa_. Heather Deegan. 2000. 256 pages. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [0-582-38227-0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-582-38227-0 "Special:BookSources/0-582-38227-0").
- _Twentieth-Century South Africa_. William Beinart [Oxford University Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford_University_Press "Oxford University Press") 2001, 414 pages, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [0-19-289318-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-19-289318-1 "Special:BookSources/0-19-289318-1").
